[India's relationship with the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India-USA_relations) improved after Narendra Modi became PM.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHall2020283–285Gupta20193–9Madan202065-386] During the run-up to the general election, there was wide-ranging scepticism about future of the strategic bilateral relationship under Modi's premiership; in 2005, while [Chief Minister of Gujarat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Minister_of_Gujarat), Modi was denied a US visa during the [presidency of George W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_George_W._Bush) for his poor human-rights record.[^cite_note-Mann-165][^cite_note-Times_of_India_2005-03-18-166] Sensing Modi's victory well before the election, [US Ambassador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Ambassador_to_India) to India [Nancy Powell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nancy_Powell) contacted Modi as part of greater *[rapprochement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapprochement)* from the West. Following Modi's 2014 appointment as India's PM, [President Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Barack_Obama) congratulated him over the telephone and invited Modi to visit the US.[^cite_note-387] The Modi government enjoyed a positive relationship with the US during the presidencies of Barack Obama and his successor [Donald Trump](4848272.md).[^cite_note-388][^cite_note-389]



Modi and Russian president [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) in Moscow, Russia, 9 July 2024

During the first few months after his appointment as PM, Modi visited a number of countries in support of his policy, and attended the [BRICS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BRICS), [ASEAN](106539.md) and [G20](2206608.md) summits.[^cite_note-Hall-383] One of Modi's first visits as PM was to Nepal, during which he promised one billion US dollars in aid.[^cite_note-390] Modi also made several visits to the US;[^cite_note-Pant-391] this was described as an unexpected development because of the US's earlier denial of a US travel visa to Modi over his role in the 2002 Gujarat riots. The visits were expected to strengthen diplomatic and trade relations between the two countries.[^cite_note-Pant-391]

In 2015, the Indian parliament ratified a land-exchange deal with Bangladesh in the [India–Bangladesh enclaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India%E2%80%93Bangladesh_enclaves), which the government of Manmohan Singh had initiated.[^cite_note-Sen-254] Modi's administration brought renewed attention to India's "Look East Policy", which was instituted in 1991. The policy, which was renamed the "Act East Policy", involved directing Indian foreign policy towards East Asia and [Southeast Asia](28741.md).[^cite_note-Downie-392] The government signed agreements to improve land connectivity with [Myanmar](19457.md) through the Indian state of [Manipur](422642.md); this represented a break with India's historic engagement with Myanmar, which prioritised border security over trade.[^cite_note-Downie-392] [China–India relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China%E2%80%93India_relations) rapidly deteriorated following the [2020 China–India skirmishes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_China%E2%80%93India_skirmishes).[^cite_note-393] Modi pledged aid of \$900 million to Afghanistan, which he visited twice and was honoured with Afghanistan's highest civilian honour in 2016.[^cite_note-394][^cite_note-395] In September 2022, Modi appeared to have developed a strong personal relationship with Russia's president [Vladimir Putin](32817.md).[^cite_note-396][^cite_note-397][^cite_note-398]



&#x20;Modi with Russian president [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) and Chinese president [Xi Jinping](2017814.md)

India hosted the [2023 G20 New Delhi summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_G20_New_Delhi_summit), during which the [African Union](10634933.md) joined the G20 as a permanent member.[^cite_note-399] In an interview on 26 August 2023, Prime Minister Modi expressed optimism about the G20 countries' evolving agenda under India's presidency, shifting toward a human-centric development approach that aligns with the concerns of the [Global South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_South), including addressing [climate change](5042951.md), [debt restructuring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Debt_restructuring) through the G20's Common Framework for Debt, and a strategy for regulation of global [cryptocurrencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cryptocurrencies).[^cite_note-400][^cite_note-401][^cite_note-402] Modi's government faced scrutiny in the lead-up to the G20 meeting as multiple news sources reported that Indian authorities demolished [slum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slum) neighbourhoods in [New Delhi](51585.md), displacing marginalised residents.[^cite_note-403][^cite_note-404][^cite_note-405]

### Defence



[Benjamin Netanyahu](56472.md), the [prime minister of Israel](24898.md), and Modi visiting the Technology Exhibition, at [Tel Aviv](31453.md), Israel in 2017

India's nominal military spending steadily increased under Modi.[^cite_note-406] During Modi's tenure, the military budget declined, both as a fraction of GDP and when adjusted for inflation.[^cite_note-Rajagopalan-407][^cite_note-Economist_2018-03-28-408] A substantial portion of the military budget was devoted to personnel costs. Commentators wrote the budget was constraining Indian military modernisation.[^cite_note-Rajagopalan-407][^cite_note-409][^cite_note-Economist_2018-03-28-408]

Under Modi, India launched military modernisation initiative aimed at strengthening defence preparedness and streamlining defence acquisition.[^cite_note-410] Modi launched new policies under the "[Aatmanirbhar Bharat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmanirbhar_Bharat)" campaign, promoting indigenous defence manufacturing with policies to procure key weapon systems domestically. The government has implemented several of the recommendations from the Shekatkar Committee to streamline defence procurement and rationalise spending. Efforts have been made to establish Integrated Theatre Commands (ITCs) to enhance jointness among the services, although challenges persist in their full operationalisation.[^cite_note-orf2023-411]

Modi promised to be "tough on Pakistan" during his election campaign and repeatedly called Pakistan an [exporter of terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_and_state-sponsored_terrorism).[^cite_note-412][^cite_note-413][^cite_note-414] On 29 September 2016, the Modi administration said the Indian Army had conducted a [surgical strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Indian_Line_of_Control_strike) on terror [launch pads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Launch_pad) in [Azad Kashmir](2745.md); the Indian media said up to 50 terrorists and Pakistani soldiers had been killed in the strike.[^cite_note-415][^cite_note-416][^cite_note-417] Independent analysts said India's statement about the scope of the strike and the number of casualties had been exaggerated.[^cite_note-418][^cite_note-419][^cite_note-420][^cite_note-421] Pakistan denied any surgical strikes to have taken place.[^cite_note-422] In February 2019, India carried out [airstrikes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Balakot_airstrike) against a supposed terrorist camp in Pakistan; open source satellite imagery suggested no targets of significance were hit.[^cite_note-423][^cite_note-424] [Further military skirmishes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India%E2%80%93Pakistan_border_skirmishes_\(2019\)), including cross-border shelling and the loss of an Indian aircraft, occurred.[^cite_note-425][^cite_note-426][^cite_note-427] Eight months after the incident, the Modi administration admitted that six Indian military personnel had been killed by [friendly fire](202484.md).[^cite_note-428]



Modi during his visit to the [Adampur Air Force Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adampur_Air_Force_Station), 2025.

In May 2020, Chinese and Indian troops engaged in aggressive skirmishes along the [Sino-Indian border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Indian_border), including near the disputed [Pangong Lake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pangong_Lake), [Ladakh](303611.md), and the [Tibet Autonomous Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibet_Autonomous_Region) and near the border between [Sikkim](140372.md) and the [Tibet Autonomous Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibet_Autonomous_Region). Additional clashes took place in eastern Ladakh along the [Line of Actual Control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_of_Actual_Control) (LAC).[^cite_note-429] In 2020, there were high-profile [skirmishes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_India-China_skirmishes) between the nations.[^cite_note-430] A series of talks between India and China were held.[^cite_note-431] The first border clash reported in 2021 was on 20 January; this was referred to as a minor border clash in Sikkim.[^cite_note-432] Modi was from late 2022 criticised for maintaining silence over the [ceding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020%E2%80%932021_China%E2%80%93India_skirmishes#India's_territorial_loss) of about 2,000 km<sup>2</sup> land to China since June 2020.[^cite_note-433][^cite_note-434][^cite_note-435]

In December 2021, Modi signed an agreement with Russian president [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) to extend military technical cooperation.[^cite_note-Siddiqui-436] The Modi government bought the [S-400 missile system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S-400_missile_system), an anti-missile striking system, strengthening the relationship between the two nations.[^cite_note-437] India refused to condemn the [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md) and stayed neutral.[^cite_note-Siddiqui-436][^cite_note-438] The Indian government's [Operation Ganga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Ganga) initiative sought to return Indians stranded in Ukraine during the war. More than 19,000 Indian nationals were evacuated,[^cite_note-439][^cite_note-440] including some from neighbouring countries.[^cite_note-441]

In April 2025, [a terrorist attack in Pahalgam](79778862.md) targeting Hindu tourists by [The Resistance Front](74821509.md), an offshoot of the Pakistan-based militant organisation [Lashkar-e-Taiba](649718.md), killed 26 civilians.[^cite_note-442][^cite_note-443] The high civilian casualties of the attack, mainly consisting of Hindu men, and its location at a popular tourist destination, was aimed at embarrassing the Modi government and elicit a reaction, and created tremendous pressure within India for the government to respond sternly.[^cite_note-444][^cite_note-445] The Modi government accused Pakistan of supporting cross-border terrorism and suspended the [Indus Waters Treaty](3603967.md) and all bilateral trade. Pakistan rejected the accusation and in response suspended the [Simla Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simla_Agreement) and all trade with India. On 7 May, India launched [Operation Sindoor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Sindoor)*,* against terror launch pads used by the [Jaish-e-Mohammed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaish-e-Mohammed), [Lashkar-e-Taiba](649718.md), and [Hizbul Mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hizbul_Mujahideen) in Pakistan-administered [Azad Kashmir](2745.md) and [Pakistani Punjab](24751.md).[^cite_note-TheDiplomat_Lashkar-446][^cite_note-447] On 10 May, Pakistan retaliated by targeting Indian military sites along the border. Following a further increase in hostilities, the countries agreed to a ceasefire the same day and ended the conflict.[^cite_note-a616-448]

### Environment



Modi (right) at [CoP21 Climate Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CoP21_Climate_Conference), in Paris, announcing the founding of the [International Solar Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Solar_Alliance) (ISA). November 2015.

While naming his cabinet, Modi renamed the Ministry of Environment and Forests the "[Ministry of Environment, Forests, and Climate Change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Environment,_Forest_and_Climate_Change)", and reduced its budget allocation by more than half in his administration's first budget.[^cite_note-449] The new ministry removed or diluted a number of laws related to environmental protection, and others related to industrial activity.[^cite_note-Ruparelia-257] The government also tried to reconstitute the [National Board for Wildlife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Board_for_Wildlife) so it would no longer have representatives from NGOs but the [Supreme Court of India](395852.md) blocked this move.[^cite_note-Kothari-450] Other changes included a reduction of ministry oversight on small mining projects and ending the requirement for approval from tribal councils for projects inside forested areas. Modi also lifted a moratorium on new industrial activity in India's most-polluted areas.[^cite_note-Kothari-450] The changes were welcomed by businesspeople but criticised by environmentalists.[^cite_note-451]

Speaking with [Assamese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assamese_people) students in 2014, Modi downplayed [climate change](5042951.md), saying, "Climate has not changed. We have changed. Our habits have changed. Our habits have got spoiled. Due to that, we have destroyed our entire environment."[^cite_note-452] Later in his administration, however, he has called for [climate action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_action),[^cite_note-453][^cite_note-454] especially with the proliferation of [clean energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clean_energy).[^cite_note-455][^cite_note-456] In 2015, Modi proposed the [International Solar Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Solar_Alliance) initiative to encourage investment in solar energy.[^cite_note-457] Holding developed countries responsible,[^cite_note-458] Modi and his government have said India has had a negligible historical role in climate change. At the [COP26](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COP26) conference, Modi announced India would target [carbon neutrality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_neutrality) by 2070 and expand its renewable energy capacity.[^cite_note-459] Indian environmentalists and economists applauded the decision, describing it as bold climate action.[^cite_note-460] India has become the only major economy to be on track to meet its [Paris Agreement](30242372.md) goals.[^cite_note-461] It has achieved 10 per cent of ethanol blending five months ahead of schedule.[^cite_note-462]

### Democratic backsliding

Main article: [Premiership of Narendra Modi § Democratic backsliding](52507144.md#Democratic_backsliding)

See also: [Democratic backsliding in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_backsliding_in_India), [Internet censorship in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_censorship_in_India), [Godi media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Godi_media), and [Protests against Narendra Modi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protests_against_Narendra_Modi)

Under Modi's tenure, India has experienced [democratic backsliding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_backsliding).[^cite_note-lo9-22] His rule is known for weakening of democratic institutions, [individual rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Individual_rights), and [freedom of expression](21401843.md).[^cite_note-463][^cite_note-464] According to one study, "The BJP government incrementally but systemically attacked nearly all existing mechanisms that are in place to hold the political executive to account, either by ensuring that these mechanisms became subservient to the political executive or were captured by party loyalists".[^cite_note-Khaitan-465][^cite_note-466] The Modi government has used state power to intimidate and stifle critics in the media and academia, undermining freedom of expression and alternative sources of information.[^cite_note-467][^cite_note-Ganguly_2020-09-468] His administration has been criticised for using a democratic mandate to undermine democratic processes, including focusing on Hindu-nationalist priorities rather than economic development. Modi's second and third term as PM, in particular, saw the erosion of civil rights and [press freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_the_press_in_India).[^cite_note-469][^cite_note-u259-470]

Modi is the longest-serving prime minister outside the [Indian National Congress](149333.md),[^cite_note-471] and has received [consistently high approval ratings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opinion_polling_on_the_Narendra_Modi_premiership) during his premiership.[^cite_note-Kaul2-472][^cite_note-473][^cite_note-474]

### Image

Modi is considered to be a [vegetarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vegetarianism) and a [teetotaller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teetotalism).[^cite_note-475][^cite_note-476] On 31 August 2012, he posted on [Google Hangouts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Google_Hangouts), becoming the first Indian politician to interact with citizens on a live chat.[^cite_note-477][^cite_note-478] Modi regularly wears an ironed, half-sleeved *[kurta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurta)*, and his suit has his name repeatedly embroidered in the pinstripes, which he wore during a state visit by US president [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama), which drew public and media attention, and criticism.[^cite_note-479][^cite_note-480][^cite_note-481] Scholars and biographers have described Modi's personality as energetic, eccentric, arrogant, and charismatic.[^cite_note-Stepan-30][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarino201460–70-482]

The nomination of Modi for the prime-ministership drew attention to his reputation as "one of contemporary India's most controversial and divisive politicians".[^cite_note-Basu-190][^cite_note-483][^cite_note-484] During the 2014 election campaign, the BJP projected an image of Modi as a strong, masculine leader who would be able to take difficult decisions.[^cite_note-Basu-190][^cite_note-Chhibber-203][^cite_note-Jaffrelot_2015-06-208][^cite_note-Chacko-213][^cite_note-Srivastava-214] Campaigns in which he has participated have focused on Modi as an individual, an unusual tactic for the BJP and RSS.[^cite_note-Jaffrelot_2015-06-208] Modi has relied upon his reputation as a politician able to bring about economic growth and development.[^cite_note-485] Modi's role in the 2002 Gujarat riots continues to attract criticism and controversy.[^cite_note-Buncombe2-486] Modi's hardline Hindutva ideology and the policies adopted by his government continue to draw criticism, and have been seen as evidence of a majoritarian and exclusionary social agenda.[^cite_note-Buncombe-28][^cite_note-Stepan-30][^cite_note-Manor-162][^cite_note-Jaffrelot_2015-06-208]

### Approval ratings

Main article: [Opinion polling on the Narendra Modi premiership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opinion_polling_on_the_Narendra_Modi_premiership)



Modi interacting with the school children after delivering his address on [Independence Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Day_\(India\)) in New Delhi, 15 August 2017

During his premiership, Modi has received consistently high approval ratings; at the end of his first year in office, he received an overall approval rating of 87% in a [Pew Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research) poll, with 68% of respondents rating him "very favourably" and 93% approving of his government.[^cite_note-487] Modi's approval rating remained largely consistent at around 74% during his second year in office, according to a nationwide poll conducted by instaVaani.[^cite_note-488] At the end of his second year in office, an updated Pew Research poll showed Modi continued to receive high overall approval ratings of 81%, with 57% of those polled rating him "very favourably".[^cite_note-489][^cite_note-490] At the end of his third year in office, a further Pew Research poll showed Modi with an overall approval rating of 88%, his highest yet, with 69% of people polled rating him "very favourably".[^cite_note-491] A poll conducted by *[The Times of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Times_of_India)* in May 2017 showed 77% of respondents rated Modi as "very good" and "good".[^cite_note-492] In early 2017, a survey by Pew Research Center showed Modi to be the most popular figure in Indian politics.[^cite_note-493] In an analysis by [Morning Consult](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morning_Consult), Modi had the highest net approval rating of all government leaders in the 13 countries being tracked as of 22 December 2020.[^cite_note-494][^cite_note-495]

### In popular culture

See also: [Category\:Cultural depictions of Narendra Modi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Cultural_depictions_of_Narendra_Modi)

*Modi Kaka Ka Gaon* (Modi uncle's town), a 2017 Indian [Hindi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindi)-language drama film by Tushar Amrish Goel, is the first [biopic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biopic) about Modi. It stars Vikas Mahante in the titular role.[^cite_note-496] *[PM Narendra Modi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PM_Narendra_Modi_\(film\))*, a 2019 Hindi-language biographical [propaganda film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propaganda_film) by [Omung Kumar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omung_Kumar), stars [Vivek Oberoi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vivek_Oberoi) in the titular role and covers Modi's rise to the premiership.[^cite_note-497] An Indian web series called *[Modi: Journey of a Common Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modi:_Journey_of_a_Common_Man)*, which is based on the same premise, was released in May 2019 on the [video streaming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Video_streaming) platform [Eros Now](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eros_Now) with [Ashish Sharma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashish_Sharma) portraying Modi.[^cite_note-498]

[*7 RCR*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/7_RCR_\(TV_series\)) (*[7, Race Course Road](547114.md)*), a 2014 Indian [docudrama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Docudrama) political television series which charts the political careers of prominent Indian politicians, covered Modi's rise to the PM's office in the episodes "Story of Narendra Modi from 1950 to 2001", "Story of Narendra Modi in Controversial Years from 2001 to 2013", "Truth Behind Brand Modi", "Election Journey of Narendra Modi to 7 RCR", and "Masterplan of Narendra Modi's NDA Govt", with Sangam Rai in the role of Modi.[^cite_note-499] *[India: The Modi Question](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India:_The_Modi_Question)*, a 2023 [BBC](19344654.md) documentary, examines Modi's role in the 2002 Gujarat violence and his record as Prime Minister of India. The Indian government banned the documentary, drawing widespread criticism.[^cite_note-500][^cite_note-501][^cite_note-502][^cite_note-503]

Other portrayals of Modi include those by [Rajit Kapur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajit_Kapur) in the film *[Uri: The Surgical Strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uri:_The_Surgical_Strike)* (2019) and [Vikram Gokhale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vikram_Gokhale) in the web-television series *[Avrodh: The Siege Within](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avrodh:_The_Siege_Within)* (2020), both of which are based on the [2016 Uri attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Uri_attack) and the [subsequent Indian surgical strikes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Indian_Line_of_Control_strike).[^cite_note-504][^cite_note-505] Gokhale reprised the role in the sequel *[Avrodh: The Siege Within 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avrodh:_The_Siege_Within_2)* (2022)*,* which is based on the [2016 Indian banknote demonetisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Indian_banknote_demonetisation).[^cite_note-506] Pratap Singh played a character based on Modi in *[Chand Bujh Gaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chand_Bujh_Gaya)* (2005) which is set against the backdrop of the Gujarat riots.[^cite_note-507]

Modi appeared in [an episode](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Man_vs._Wild#Special_India_episodes) of [Discovery Channel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discovery_Channel_India)'s show *[Man vs. Wild](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Man_vs._Wild)* with the host [Bear Grylls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bear_Grylls) in July 2019,[^cite_note-508][^cite_note-509] becoming the second world leader after Barack Obama to appear in the reality show.[^cite_note-510] In the show, Modi treks through jungles, and talks about nature and wildlife conservation with Grylls.[^cite_note-511] The episode was recorded in [Jim Corbett National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Corbett_National_Park), [Uttarakhand](1429154.md), and was broadcast in India and 180 other countries.[^cite_note-512] Modi hosts *[Mann Ki Baat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mann_Ki_Baat)*, a monthly radio programme on [All India Radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_India_Radio), and has conducted the competition *[Pariksha Pe Charcha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pariksha_Pe_Charcha)*, and discussions for students and the issues they face in examinations.[^cite_note-513][^cite_note-514]

## Awards and recognition

Main articles: [List of awards and honours received by Narendra Modi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_awards_and_honours_received_by_Narendra_Modi) and [List of things named after Narendra Modi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_things_named_after_Narendra_Modi)

In March 2012 and June 2014, Narendra Modi appeared on the cover of the Asian edition of *[Time Magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_\(magazine\))*, becoming one of the few Indian politicians to have done so.[^cite_note-515][^cite_note-516] In 2014, *[CNN-News18](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CNN-News18)* (formally *CNN-IBN*) news network awarded Modi [Indian of the Year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CNN%E2%80%93News18_Indian_of_the_Year).[^cite_note-517] In June 2015, Modi was featured on the [cover of Time Magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_covers_of_Time_magazine_\(2010s\)).[^cite_note-518] In 2014, 2015, 2017, 2020 and 2021, he was named one of Time magazine's [100 Most Influential People in the World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_100).[^cite_note-519] In its [list of the world's most powerful people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbes_list_of_the_World's_Most_Powerful_People), *[Forbes Magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbes_Magazine)* ranked Modi 15th in 2014 and 9th in 2015, 2016 and 2018.[^cite_note-520] In 2015, Modi was ranked the [13th Most Influential Person in the World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/50_Most_Influential_\(Bloomberg_Markets_ranking\)) by *[Bloomberg Markets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bloomberg_Markets) Magazine*.[^cite_note-521] In 2021, *Time* called Modi the third "pivotal leader" of independent India after [Jawaharlal Nehru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru) and [Indira Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indira_Gandhi), who "dominated the country's politics like no one since them".[^cite_note-522][^cite_note-523] Modi was ranked fifth on *[Fortune Magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortune_\(magazine\))*'s first annual list of the "World's Greatest Leaders" in 2015.[^cite_note-524][^cite_note-525] In 2017, [Gallup International Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup_International_Association) (GIA) conducted a poll and ranked Modi third-top leader of the world.[^cite_note-526][^cite_note-527] In 2016, a wax statue of Modi was unveiled at [Madame Tussauds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madame_Tussauds) [wax museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wax_museum) in [London](17867.md).[^cite_note-528][^cite_note-529]



Narendra Modi's wax statue at [Madame Tussauds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madame_Tussauds), London

In 2015, Modi was named one of *Time*'s "30 Most Influential People on the Internet" because he was the [second-most-followed politician](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_most-followed_Twitter_accounts) on [Twitter](9988187.md) and [Facebook](7529378.md).[^cite_note-530] In 2018, he was the third-most-followed world leader on Twitter and the [most-followed world leader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_most-followed_Instagram_accounts) on [Instagram](31591547.md) and Facebook.[^cite_note-531][^cite_note-532][^cite_note-533] In October 2018, Modi received United Nations' highest environmental award, the [Champions of the Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Champions_of_the_Earth), for policy leadership by "pioneering work in championing" the [International Solar Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Solar_Alliance) and "new areas of levels of cooperation on environmental action".[^cite_note-534][^cite_note-535] Modi was conferred the 2018 [Seoul Peace Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seoul_Peace_Prize).[^cite_note-536][^cite_note-537]

Following his [second oath of office ceremony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_oath_of_office_ceremony_of_Narendra_Modi) as Prime Minister of India, a picture of Modi was displayed on the facade of the [Abu Dhabi National Oil Company](7992255.md) (ADNOC) building in [Abu Dhabi, United Arab Emirates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Dhabi,_United_Arab_Emirates).[^cite_note-538] The Texas India Forum hosted a community event, *[Howdy Modi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howdy_Modi)*, in honour of Modi on 22 September 2019 at [NRG Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NRG_Stadium) in [Houston, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston,_Texas). The event was attended by over 50,000 people and several American politicians, including President [Donald Trump](4848272.md), making it the largest gathering for an invited [foreign leader visiting the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_diplomatic_visits_to_the_United_States) other than the [Pope](23056.md).[^cite_note-539][^cite_note-540] At the event, Modi was presented with the [Key to the City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Keys_to_the_City_in_the_United_States#Texas) of Houston by Mayor [Sylvester Turner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sylvester_Turner).[^cite_note-541] The [Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_&_Melinda_Gates_Foundation) awarded Modi the [Global Goalkeeper Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goalkeepers_\(Gates_Foundation\)) on 24 September 2019 in New York City, in recognition of the [Swachh Bharat Mission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swachh_Bharat_Mission) and "the progress India has made in providing safe sanitation under his leadership".[^cite_note-542][^cite_note-543][^cite_note-544]

In 2020, Modi was among eight world leaders who were awarded the parody [Ig Nobel Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ig_Nobel_Prize) in Medical Education "for using the [COVID-19 viral pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_India) to teach the world that politicians can have a more immediate effect on life and death than scientists and doctors can".[^cite_note-545] On 21 December 2020, US president Donald Trump awarded Modi the [Legion of Merit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legion_of_Merit) for improving [India–United States relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India%E2%80%93United_States_relations).[^cite_note-546][^cite_note-547][^cite_note-548] On 24 February 2021, [Gujarat Cricket Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gujarat_Cricket_Association) controversially renamed Motera Stadium in [Ahmedabad](206337.md)—[the largest cricket stadium in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cricket_grounds_by_capacity)—[Narendra Modi Stadium](80201506.md).[^cite_note-549]

Modi has [received several state honours](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_awards_and_honours_received_by_Narendra_Modi). In March 2024, the [King of Bhutan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Bhutan) conferred Modi with the [Order of the Dragon King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_the_Dragon_King), the highest decoration of Bhutan. It was the first such award to a non-Bhutanese head of government.[^cite_note-550] In July 2024, during a visit to Russia, Modi was awarded the [Order of St. Andrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_St._Andrew), Russia's highest civilian award.[^cite_note-551] In November 2024, during a diplomatic visit to Nigeria, Modi was conferred the [Grand Commander of the Order of the Niger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Commander_of_the_Order_of_the_Niger) by the Nigerian president. He was the second foreign dignitary to receive the award, after [Queen Elizabeth II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Elizabeth_II).[^cite_note-552][^cite_note-553] Modi was also presented the symbolic "Key to the City" of [Abuja](56823.md) by Minister [Nyesom Wike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nyesom_Wike).[^cite_note-554][^cite_note-555] In December 2024, Modi was conferred the [Order of Mubarak the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_Mubarak_the_Great), the highest national honour of [Kuwait](7515890.md).[^cite_note-556][^cite_note-557] Modi was named a [Grand Commander of the Order of the Star and Key](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_the_Star_and_Key_of_the_Indian_Ocean), the highest honour conferred by [Mauritius](19201.md), in March 2025.[^cite_note-558][^cite_note-559] In April 2025, Modi was awarded the [Sri Lanka Mitra Vibhushana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sri_Lanka_Mitra_Vibhushana), the highest Sri Lankan award to a foreign leader.[^cite_note-560] On 25 February 2026, Prime Minister Narendra Modi became the first recipient of the [Medal of the Knesset](82512098.md), the highest parliamentary honour of [Israel](9282173.md).[^cite_note-561] On 17 May 2026, Modi was appointed a Commander Grand Cross of the [Order of the Polar Star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_the_Polar_Star), Sweden's second-highest civilian honour.[^cite_note-562] On 18 May 2026, Prime Minister Narendra Modi was conferred the Grand Cross of the [Royal Norwegian Order of Merit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Norwegian_Order_of_Merit), the highest honor awarded by Norway to foreign nationals.[^cite_note-563]

## Writing career

In 2008, Modi published a Gujarati book titled *Jyotipunj*, which contains profiles of RSS leaders. The longest was of [M. S. Golwalkar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M._S._Golwalkar), under whose leadership the RSS expanded and whom Modi refers to as *Pujniya Shri Guruji* ("Guru worthy of worship").[^cite_note-571] According to *[The Economic Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economic_Times)*, Modi's intention was to explain the workings of the RSS to his readers, and to reassure RSS members he remained ideologically aligned with them.

After becoming the prime minister he also authored a book called [Exam Warriors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exam_Warriors), a guide for children to commendably face exams. Modi has written eight other books, mostly containing short stories for children.[^cite_note-572] Modi has penned the lyrics of two Gujarati [garba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garba_\(dance\)) songs; *Garbo*, sung by [Dhvani Bhanushali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhvani_Bhanushali) and composed by [Tanishk Bagchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanishk_Bagchi), and *Maadi*, sung by [Divya Kumar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divya_Kumar_\(singer\)) and composed by [Meet Bros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meet_Bros). The songs were released on the occasion of autumn [Navratri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navratri) in 2023.[^cite_note-573][^cite_note-574]

*[Abundance in Millets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abundance_in_Millets)*, a song by [Falu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falu) and Gaurav Shah that featured a speech given by Modi for the promotion of [millet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millet), received a nomination in the [Best Global Music Performance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grammy_Award_for_Best_Global_Music_Performance) category for the [2024 Grammy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/66th_Annual_Grammy_Awards).[^cite_note-575][^cite_note-576]

[^cite_note-4]: &#x20;[Gujarati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gujarati_language) pronunciation: [\[ˈnəɾendɾə dɑmodəɾˈdɑs ˈmodiː\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Gujarati).\
    Modi's middle name at birth, *Damodardas*, is his father's first name, following the [Gujarati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gujarati_people) tradition of [patronymics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patronymic); while Modi himself regularly uses the name, he is widely known as simply *Narendra Modi*.<sup>[\[3\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarino20144–5-3)</sup>

As of 10 June 2026, Modi had served 4399 days as prime minister.<sup>[\[4\]](#cite_note-j963-5)</sup><sup>[\[5\]](#cite_note-y386-6)</sup><sup>[\[6\]](#cite_note-c328-7)</sup>

[^cite_note-foo-39]: &#x20;*Narendra Modi* was born *Narendra Damodardas Modi* on 17 September 1950. He uses *Damodardas* as his middle name—[Gujaratis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gujarati_people) have a tradition of [using the names of their fathers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patronymic) as their middle names—but he is widely known as *Narendra Modi*.<sup>[\[3\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarino20144–5-3)</sup>

[^cite_note-74]: &#x20;Applications were filed with the [Prime Minister's Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister's_Office_\(India\)) (PMO) under the [RTI Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_to_Information_Act,_2005) seeking details of his arrest. In reply, the PMO said it maintains official records on Modi only since he became the prime minister in 2014. Despite this claim, the official website of the PMO contains information about Modi from the 1950s.<sup>[\[62\]](#cite_note-72)</sup><sup>[\[63\]](#cite_note-73)</sup>

[^cite_note-85]: &#x20;Replying to an RTI query, the School of Open Learning said it did not have any data of students who received a BA degree in 1978.<sup>[\[64\]](#cite_note-India_Today_2017-03-14-75)</sup> Jayantibhai Patel, a former political science professor of Gujarat University, said the subjects listed in Modi's MA degree were not offered by the university when Modi was studying there.<sup>[\[71\]](#cite_note-82)</sup><sup>[\[72\]](#cite_note-83)</sup> In 2016, [Delhi University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi_University) deemed the BA degree to be authentic.<sup>[\[73\]](#cite_note-84)</sup>

[^cite_note-103]: &#x20;The party is commonly referred to simply as 'Congress.'

[^cite_note-114]: &#x20;The exact number of people killed in the train burning is variously reported. For example, the [BBC](19344654.md) says it was 59,<sup>[\[99\]](#cite_note-112)</sup> while *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)* put the figure at 60.<sup>[\[100\]](#cite_note-113)</sup>

[^cite_note-184]: &#x20;By December 2008, 500,000 structures had been built, of which 113,738 were [check dams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Check_dam), which helped recharge the aquifers beneath them.<sup>[\[168\]](#cite_note-Shah_2011-182)</sup> Sixty of the 112 [tehsils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehsil) which had depleted the [water table](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_table) in 2004 had regained their normal groundwater levels by 2010.<sup>[\[169\]](#cite_note-183)</sup>

[^cite_note-194]: &#x20;Under Modi, Gujarat topped the [World Bank](36752.md)'s "ease of doing business" rankings among Indian states for two consecutive years.<sup>[\[177\]](#cite_note-192)</sup> In 2013, a report measuring governance, growth, citizens' rights, and labour and business regulation among the country's 20 largest states, ranked Gujarat first among Indian states for "economic freedom".<sup>[\[172\]](#cite_note-Schöttli-187)</sup><sup>[\[178\]](#cite_note-193)</sup>

[^cite_note-198]: &#x20;In 2013, Gujarat ranked 13th in India with respect to rates of poverty, and 21st in education. Nearly 45 per cent of children under five were underweight and 23 per cent were undernourished, putting the state in the "alarming" category on the [India State Hunger Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India_State_Hunger_Index).<sup>[\[179\]](#cite_note-195)</sup><sup>[\[180\]](#cite_note-Harriss-196)</sup> A study by [UNICEF](19698110.md) and the Indian government found Gujarat under Modi had a poor record in [immunisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immunisation) of children.<sup>[\[181\]](#cite_note-Economist_2015-06-27-197)</sup>

[^cite_note-199]: &#x20;From 2001 to 2011, Gujarat did not change its position relative to the rest of the country with respect to poverty and female literacy, remaining near the median of the 29 Indian states.<sup>[\[118\]](#cite_note-Jaffrelot_2016-01-16-132)</sup> It showed a marginal improvement in rates of infant mortality and its position with respect to individual consumption declined.<sup>[\[118\]](#cite_note-Jaffrelot_2016-01-16-132)</sup> The quality of education in government schools in Gujarat ranked below that of many Indian states.<sup>[\[118\]](#cite_note-Jaffrelot_2016-01-16-132)</sup>

[^cite_note-385]: &#x20;"The Narendra Modi led government completed two years in power in May 2016 and the prime minister has made his mark on both the domestic and foreign policy fronts. It is important to assess how successful his initiatives have been in the arena of foreign affairs in comparison to his predecessors. In this regard, this paper identifies and examines the key trends and issues in foreign policy under the Modi led administration and the measures needed to translate speeches and policies into action. Modi government has also taken a serious node of relations with middle-east nations, as well as Iran and Israel."<sup>[\[366\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHall2016278–281-384)</sup>

The masterplot of love jihad is not just literary imaginings but also a potent brew of Islamophobia and patriarchy that harms Muslims and women. Akin to some of the post-9/11 rhetoric in the United States, contemporary Hindu nationalists propagate "a mythical history of medieval Muslim tyranny and present-day existential threat, demanding mobilization and revenge."

[^cite_note-278]: &#x20;Sharma, Ajita (1 April 2020). "Afrazul's murder: Law and love jihad". *Jindal Global Law Review*. **11** (1). Springer: 77–95. [S2CID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S2CID_\(identifier\)) [220512241](https://api.semanticscholar.org/CorpusID\:220512241). The fake claim by the Hindu right-wing that love jihad forces Hindu women to love and marry a Muslim man and convert to Islam is perpetuating an already existing anti-Muslim narrative in the country. The love jihad phenomenon has thus become a tool of hate and anger towards Muslims. Afrazul's killing by Raigher is an extreme demonstration of this form of hate and anger towards Muslims.

[^cite_note-279]: &#x20;Upadhyay, Nishant (18 May 2020). Heterosexual couples who defy caste and religious structures often face violence, some of which results in death through honor killings and lynching targeting specifically Muslim and Dalit men. For instance, the Hindutva campaign against what it calls the "love jihad" is an attempt to protect Hindu women from Muslim men, as the latter are imagined/blamed to convert Hindu women to Islam through trickery and marriage (Gupta 2018b, 85). Needless to say, these claims are unfounded and Islamophobic imaginations of the Hindu Right.

Muslims form about 15% of India's population and have suffered severe marginalization in education and employment, since the partition of Hindu-majority India and Muslim-majority Pakistan in 1947 (Alam, 2010). They have since faced recurrent riots (Varshney, 2003). Other hostilities include false accusations of love jihad (a conspiracy theory claiming Muslim men feign love with non-Muslim women to convert them to Islam) and attempts to convert Muslims to Hinduism by Hindu fundamentalist organizations (Gupta, 2009).

The Resistance Front conducted limited attacks in Kashmir until April 2025, when it killed 26 tourists in Pahalgam, an attack that resulted in India and Pakistan's conflict in May 2025.

[^cite_note-443]: &#x20;[Ganguly, Sumit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumit_Ganguly); Warren, Spenser A. (2025), "Escalation Risks Rising? Airpower in Kargil and Pahalgam", *[The Washington Quarterly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Washington_Quarterly)*, **48** (3): 127–143, India's 2025 strikes against Pakistan were a reaction to a terrorist attack on April 22, when members of The Resistance Front (TRF), also called Kashmir Resistance, attacked tourists near the town of Pahalgam in the Indian-administered portion of the state of Jammu and Kashmir, killing twenty-six people. The attack was the deadliest terrorist assault against civilians since the November terrorist attacks in Mumbai in 2008. The TRF is an offshoot of Lashkar-e-Taiba (LeT), the group responsible for these 11/26 attacks. The LeT is based in Pakistan and has ties to elements of the Pakistani military and intelligence services. The terrorists separated men from women and children and targeted Hindu men, asking tourists to recite verses from the Quran and executing those who could not.

[^cite_note-444]: &#x20;Sen, Gaurav (May 2026), "Do Small Wars Still Stay Small? Rethinking Escalation Theory through Operation Sindoor", *[Small Wars & Insurgencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Routledge)*: 1–20, The immediate catalyst for the conflict was the terror attack on 22 April 2025, in Pahalgam, a popular tourist destination in Indian Union territory of Jammu and Kashmir (J\&K). The attack was not merely a random act of violence but a calculated provocation designed to exploit communal fault lines. Assailants linked to The Resistance Front (TRF), a proxy for Lashkar-e-Taiba (LeT), executed twenty-six civilians, targeting them based on religious identity. Eyewitness accounts suggest that the victims were asked to identify their religion before being shot, a tactic reminiscent of the darkest days of the insurgency in the 1990s. The brutality of the attack, which occurred in broad daylight in front of families, generated intense domestic pressure on the Indian government to act, effectively closing the space for diplomatic maneuvering.

[^cite_note-445]: &#x20;Hall, Ian (March 2026), "India's Foreign Policy amid Global Unpredictability", in [Ganguly, Sumit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumit_Ganguly) (ed.), [*The Hoover Institution’s Annual Survey of India 2026*](https://hoover-s3-website.s3.us-west-2.amazonaws.com/s3fs-public/research/docs/Ganguly_SurveyofIndia2026_web-260220.pdf) (PDF), [Hoover Institution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoover_Institution), p. 44, The nature and the scale of the \[Pahalgam] attack—which resulted in the highest civilian death toll since 2008—were calibrated to embarrass the Modi government and to provoke a response. Unlike the Pathankot, Uri, and Pulwama incidents, where the targets were the Indian security forces, the Pahalgam terrorists aimed at tourists enjoying a celebrated beauty spot in Jammu and Kashmir, singling out Hindu men and gunning them down. Both the targets and the location appear to have been chosen to undermine government claims that Jammu and Kashmir were now safe to visit, thanks to its efforts to secure the state and deter terrorism. It was thus inevitable that the Modi government would respond robustly to such a provocation.
